One button makes one zip file with what is needed to find out why something does not work: the logs of Kodi and of the system, the graphics and the displays, the sound, the network, the USB devices, the journal of this start and of the one before it, the newest crash log of Kodi, how long the start took, and what the diagnostics saved at the last starts. Every file goes through the filter of the diagnostics first, and nothing is sent anywhere. The file stays in `/storage/logfiles` and can be copied at once to the top of a USB drive. Tried in a virtual machine.
- **The diagnostic switch is a setting** (*Settings > LibreELEC > System > Submit Log > Save diagnostics at every start*), on a USB drive and on an internal disk alike, so the Windows installer is no longer the only way to turn it on. When it is turned on it says what it saves, where, how much and through which filter. It works from the next start. Tried in a virtual machine.
- **A player information page that a person can read.** Press O, or the red key of a TV remote that sends keys over HDMI-CEC (the red key also closes it). The first page is one panel: a sentence that says whether the video plays as intended and, if not, what is wrong and what to do; what the file carries; what Hades does with it; and what reaches your TV. The details are behind it, with every level of the Dolby Vision metadata (L1 to L6, L8 to L11, L254 and L255) and which of them are used, why HDR is not told to the display, the colour of the film itself (profile 5 is named IPT-PQ-c2), the pictures dropped and skipped, why the sound is decoded where a setting is the reason, and the decoder as "Graphics card (VAAPI)" or "Processor (software)". A stream that does not say it is HLG or HDR10 is named by its picture (an HLG stream through an add-on, found by vertox; after an idea of CroqueMr's build). Where the display has no mode at the rate of the video although *Adjust display refresh rate* is on, the advice says that, instead of asking you to turn on a setting that is on already. Its values move twice a second, so opening it over a Dolby Vision picture no longer redraws the whole interface for the values that change with every packet. The same pages are made for other skins, with their words in every language. Works on a GeForce RTX 4080 SUPER with an LG C5 (author).
- **The settings are in groups, with help in plain words.** *System > Display* has four groups with a heading each (HDR; Dolby Vision; HDR on displays without Dolby Vision, and projectors; HDR colour depth and format). Most of the settings that Hades adds are now at the Expert level, so the Advanced level shows only what is meant for everyone. Their help says what changes on the screen or in the sound, when to want it and what to do if the picture goes wrong; labels and messages no longer use internal names. The Dolby Vision settings that are about a display that takes Dolby Vision are shown only where the display declares it.
- **Samba tells you how to get in.** Turning Samba on, in the first-run wizard or in *Settings > LibreELEC > Services*, shows a window with the address, the user (`libreelec`) and the generated password, says where to read them again and how to open the share from File Explorer, and says that the same login copies an update file to the Update share. The README explains updating with a `.tar` step by step, with Samba and SSH.
- **The boot menu waits 5 seconds, and shows only once after an update.** The menu now waits 5 seconds before it starts the system by itself (10 before), as LibreELEC's BIOS menu does, and *Settings > LibreELEC > System > Boot menu* offers 10, 5, 3 and 1 second. Updating to 1.0 brings a menu that waited the old 10 seconds to 5, once; a wait that you had set to something else is kept, and from 1.0 on every update keeps the wait you choose, also an update with the Windows installer. An update restarts the PC by itself, and the PC showed the boot menu, with its wait, before the update was applied and again after the restart that ends it: the restart that applies an update now goes straight on (on a BIOS PC, holding Shift still shows the menu, which then waits as usual), and the menu is back from the next start. Tried in virtual machines, with UEFI and with BIOS.
- **A display connected while Hades runs is taken without a restart** on the same graphics card: the menu stays where you were and music keeps playing (Hades asks first, and goes there by itself after a few seconds without an answer). When another display takes the place of the one in use, its HDR, Dolby Vision, 3D and sound are known at once, not from the next video. A display on another graphics card still needs a restart. Tried in a virtual machine.
- **A notice of the playback mode, off by default.** *System > Display > Show the playback mode when a video starts* says, a moment after each video starts (the next one of a playlist too), what it plays as: for example "Dolby Vision, FEL, 3840x2160 at 23.976 Hz".
- **"Restart LibreELEC Hades" in the power menu.** Quit is hidden on LibreELEC, so the only way to restart the application was to restart the PC.
- **Clearer wording where Dolby Vision is not sent.** The notice that a display takes Dolby Vision only as Low-Latency no longer blames the game mode: some displays offer it only that way whatever the input, and for them turning the game mode off does nothing. It now says the fact and what to do (turn on *Dolby Vision for TVs in game mode (Low-Latency)*). A TV behind a DisplayPort or USB-C converter is told to use an HDMI output of the graphics instead. *Video scaling method* in the video settings now says that Dolby Vision output does not use it: the bridge scales with libplacebo's filters.
- **Nameservers of your own, also with DHCP** (*Settings > LibreELEC > Connections*, a connection, IPv4 or IPv6). Empty means the ones the network gives, and the fields show only what you set; saving no longer turns the network's nameservers into a fixed setting, and a connection no longer shows the lists of the one opened before. After an idea of damnms for CoreELEC.

**Dolby Vision**

- **Sent as composed by default.** Native Dolby Vision of profiles 7 and 8 now goes to the TV as the composer of the specification makes it, in the video's own YCbCr, bit for bit. Measured over the whole colour of every pixel, on pictures of two profile 7 films and on test patterns, it was as close to the film as IPT-PQ-c2 or closer however a TV fills in the colour between the pixels of a pair; IPT gave the right pixel of a pair a colour worked out for the left one, up to 13 to 19 delta E ITP on the edges of neon lights. Sending IPT-PQ-c2, as licensed players are reported to, stays as an expert choice (*Transport*, "As licensed players send (IPT)"). A setting left at the old default takes the new one.
- **Films whose metadata repeat a pivot play in Dolby Vision.** The Dolby Vision specification makes each pivot of a reshaping curve the one before it plus a value that may be 0. The picture check refused such metadata, so every picture of the film was refused and, after two seconds, native output gave way to HDR10. It now accepts them, as the specification does; pivots that go down are still refused. This is why films dropped to HDR10 in the reports. Tested against the specification and the metadata of the sample.
- **The TV's Dolby Vision block is no longer lost on its way from the TV.** On NVIDIA, an EDID whose extra blocks are counted by an HDMI Forum EEODB now reaches Kodi whole: the driver kept only the blocks of the base count, and a Dolby Vision block among the others was lost. On AMD and Intel, one damaged block in such an EDID no longer takes the other extra blocks out with it. The player information now tells "this TV input offers no Dolby Vision" (some TVs leave it out in their 4K 120 Hz or VRR input mode) from "the TV's EDID was read only in part", and the log names the blocks that are missing (issue #7). Checked with NVIDIA's EDID code and the kernel's tests.
- **Native Dolby Vision on the newest Ryzen processors** (display hardware DCN 4.2 and 4.2B). Each time Dolby Vision starts, the kernel reads every stage of the display pipe back from the hardware; if anything is not as Dolby Vision needs, that same video goes on as HDR10 within about two seconds (the picture holds still meanwhile), and Dolby Vision stays off until the next start. The same now holds for the Radeon RX 400, RX 500 and Vega cards.
- **Dolby Vision through a passive DisplayPort adaptor** (DP++) that identifies itself as HDMI, on Intel and AMD graphics: such an adaptor makes the port send HDMI itself, so the TV gets what an HDMI port sends. The adaptor's own limit is kept: a type 1 adaptor carries 1080p, a type 2 one up to 3840x2160 at 30 Hz. An adaptor that says DVI, or nothing, gets no Dolby Vision. NVIDIA already sent Dolby Vision through such a port.
- **The output is given back as it was found, and checked.** Native Dolby Vision changes properties of the output (its depth, range, colour format, HDR data and more). When it ends, Hades now puts back each property that is not as it found it, reads the output back and sets again what did not take, and the range and colour format are those that the settings ask for at that moment, also when you changed them during the video. A setting that the driver will not take back in that mode no longer keeps the menus from coming back. If Kodi stops during Dolby Vision, the next start puts back what the transport left.
- **Check the Dolby Vision output** (*Settings > LibreELEC > System > Submit Log*). A button that measures whether the graphics card sends the pictures of Dolby Vision exactly as Hades draws them, with the card's own check of what leaves it (a CRC). Kodi closes for about twenty seconds, the TV may show a black or noisy picture in Dolby Vision meanwhile, and Kodi comes back with the result in plain words. No setting is changed. It is meant for AMD and Intel graphics; NVIDIA's driver does not offer the measure. With the Dolby Vision path of the Radeon RX 400, RX 500 and Vega cards turned on, the diagnostics also keep that measure of 12 frames during each Dolby Vision video. Tried with the virtual display of the kernel.
- **The Dolby Vision path of the Radeon RX 400 and 500 (Polaris) and Vega cards is off unless you turn it on** (issue #20: an RX 550 with an LG G3 showed a black picture with the Dolby Vision logo). It is an expert setting now, *System > Display > Dolby Vision on this Radeon (experimental)*, shown only on those cards; Hades writes it into the boot menus and asks whether to restart. Without it, Dolby Vision plays as HDR10 there, and the log says why. The cause on that card is not found.
- **A failure of native output no longer takes Dolby Vision out of the supported HDR types.** After a video that fell back to HDR10, add-ons were told that the display had lost Dolby Vision (forum post 209039).
- **The log says why a frame is not drawn.** "Render rejected" used to name the stage whatever the check; it now names the rule. The line that gives up the direct way of drawing, the case where the interface cannot be put over the picture, and the layers that could not be imported say why, and the 10-second lines count them. An interlaced or 3D Dolby Vision video, which plays from its base layer, now says so in the log, and so does a mode with no Dolby Vision mode at its refresh rate, by its rates.
- **Videos with Dolby Vision and HDR10+ in two video streams** of one picture play the stream that *Videos with both Dolby Vision and HDR10+* chooses, and on a display that takes only one of them, that one. Before, the format that played depended on the order of the tracks.
- **HLG sent as Dolby Vision** (*System > Display > HLG as Dolby Vision*). Automatic by default: only where the output cannot send HLG at all but the display takes Dolby Vision (as the HDMI of some Intel graphics), so that the video keeps its HDR instead of being mapped to SDR. On: wherever the display takes Dolby Vision. Off: HLG always plays as HLG. The picture is converted to PQ as ITU-R BT.2100 shows an HLG picture on its reference display, and the metadata are measured from it. It works in Low-Latency too, and reads the SEI message that says a stream is HLG.
- **The trims of Dolby Vision where Hades maps the picture, as an expert choice, off by default** (*Dolby Vision: apply the trims when adjusting to your display*). Where Hades maps Dolby Vision to the display itself (Dolby Vision as HDR10 adjusted to the display, as SDR, or in Low-Latency), it can apply the level 2 trims made for exactly the peak of your display. The only published way to apply them is an example in SMPTE ST 2094-10; measured against a studio's own version of a film for a 1000-nit display, it made the shadows much darker than the studio did, so it stays off. Native Dolby Vision is not affected: the TV applies the trims itself.
- **HDR to SDR for the display you have** (*HDR to SDR: the display it is made for*, which was named for Dolby Vision only, though it always applied to HDR10, HDR10+ and HLG mapped to SDR as well): Reference (the same output as before), Bright room (white at 300 cd/m² with gamma 2.2), Projector (white at 100 cd/m²) and Custom, with the white (20 to 500 cd/m²) and the gamma (1.8 to 2.8) that you set. HLG mapped to an SDR display brighter than the reference no longer comes out about half as bright in the middle tones.
- **The black of the display** (*Black of the display*, Expert) for *Adapt Dolby Vision to my display (projectors)* and, while that is on, for *HDR10 and HDR10+ adapted to the display*: the darkest parts of the film are lifted as ITU-R BT.2390 describes, so that they do not sink into the black of a projector. 0, the default, keeps the black of the film. After an idea of CroqueMr's build.
- **The peak of the TV by hand for the automatic tone mapping (v4.0)** (*Tone mapping v4.0: set the TV's peak by hand*, Expert), for a TV that declares a wrong peak in its EDID. After an idea of YBLOD.
- **Dolby Vision pictures up to 4096x2304** are taken in and scaled down to the 3840x2160 output. Before, a 4096-wide video entered Dolby Vision, every picture was refused, and a profile 5 picture came out as its base layer in the wrong colours. A larger one plays as Kodi plays it, with a line in the log. Tried on a made-up sample.
- **The menus in Dolby Vision cannot lock you out** (issue #20). Turning on *Dolby Vision for the menus* asks you to confirm, with a 15-second countdown whose default is No, and goes back to Off unless you answer Yes.
- **Dolby Vision through the DisplayPort-to-HDMI converter of a board** (*Dolby Vision through a display converter*, Expert). The HDMI socket of many mini PCs and NUCs with Intel graphics of the 11th generation and later is behind such a converter, which Linux calls a DisplayPort output, and native output was refused there. Automatic offers it only on the boards where it was seen working (one today); On tries it on any converter; Off never. If Kodi stops during Dolby Vision, the console takes such an output back without the Dolby Vision signal. The idea and the first board are YBLOD's.
- **With NVIDIA's driver 620, Dolby Vision always goes out at 8 bits in RGB.** Driver 620 has its own settings of the depth and the colour format, which another program, or *Colour format*, may leave at another value. Native Dolby Vision now sets them to automatic while it plays and puts them back after, and the driver refuses a Dolby Vision signal at any other depth or format.
- **For add-ons: `Player.Process(video.sidedata)`,** the Dolby Vision and HDR payloads of the picture on screen, in the format and with the name that the CoreELEC builds use, so that add-ons that parse the RPU work. Nothing is collected until something reads it. `Player.Process(videooutputhdrtype)` and the new output transfer function (`videooutputeotf`, which vertox asked for) now say what the graphics driver holds, not what Kodi asked for, and are empty where the driver has no property. Tried on the host only.
- **What the conversion of a video made is given back when the video ends.** After a Dolby Vision video sent as HDR10 or HDR10+, or an HDR10 video adapted to the display, the menus went on with the conversion engaged and the graphics chip in the profile of the video. They are now released as soon as the output of the video has gone and no other video has begun.
- **The file of GPU programs no longer depends on the version of the system.** An update that changed neither the driver nor libplacebo sent the first video after it to compile every program again. The log now says how many were compiled.

**HDR and the signal to the TV**

- **HDR on a display without HDR keeps its middle tones as mastered.** The reference tone mapping, "Reference (BT.2390)", now starts compressing where ITU-R BT.2390 says: everything up to a little below the display's white goes out as mastered, and only what is brighter is squeezed in. Until now the squeezing started much lower, so faces, skin and interiors came out darker than mastered: on the reference SDR display, 100 nits of a film mastered at 1000 nits went out at 87 instead of 99, and of one mastered at 4000 nits at 61 instead of 86. The same curve, with the same fix, is used by *Dolby Vision as HDR10 mapped to the display*, *HDR10 and HDR10+ adapted to the display* and Dolby Vision Low-Latency (on a TV that shows more than the film's brightest, nothing changes). If you preferred the old look, which keeps a little more detail in the brightest highlights at the cost of darker middle tones, choose "BT.2390, libplacebo's knee" as the tone mapping method of the video, or as the *Tone mapping curve*: it is exactly the old picture. Measured with the software bridge.
- **HDR10+ adapted by its own metadata.** Where Hades adapts an HDR10+ video to the display itself (*HDR10 and HDR10+ adapted to the display*, or a display without HDR), it now follows the HDR10+ data of each scene, and the studio's tone curve where the video carries one, instead of the one peak of the whole film. In Dolby Vision Low-Latency, an HDR10+ video sent as Dolby Vision is mapped by the studio's tone curve too, and the player information says so. Measured with the software bridge.
- **Play HDR videos as SDR** (*System > Display*, Expert, Never by default): HLG videos, or HLG, HDR10 and HDR10+ videos, sent as ordinary SDR to a display that takes HDR, for a TV that shows HLG badly or a projector whose SDR picture is better. They are converted as for a display without HDR, and *Check this setup* and the player information say why. Dolby Vision videos are not affected. The display is now also told the colours that the picture is drawn in: an SDR video in BT.2020, or HDR shown as SDR with one of Kodi's own tone mapping methods, went out as BT.709 colours that the display was told were BT.2020, which a display that honours it shows too saturated.
- **HDR in 4:2:2 at 12 bits over HDMI 2.0 on Intel and AMD graphics** (*HDR in 4:2:2 at 12 bits over HDMI 2.0*, Advanced, on). At 3840x2160 and 50 or 60 Hz an HDMI 2.0 link cannot carry RGB at 10 bits, so these drivers sent HDR10, HLG and HDR10+ as YCbCr 4:2:0 at 10 bits, or as RGB at 8. Where the TV takes 4:2:2, Hades now sends them as YCbCr 4:2:2 at 12 bits inside the same 8-bit RGB signal that carries Dolby Vision, with the HDR data of the video as always: the video goes out exactly, and the menus and subtitles over it keep twice the colour detail. It is used on Intel graphics and on the AMD graphics that sent 4:2:0 there, such as the integrated graphics of the Ryzen 7000 series. Such a video starts with one more change of mode of the TV. Where the graphics cannot keep up with the frames, that mode goes back to the ordinary output for the rest of the session, and the log says why. The player information names it as the HDR10 or HLG that it carries. After an idea of YBLOD.
- **HDR above 8 bits on NVIDIA where the card would send RGB at 8, on by default** (*HDR at more than 8 bits where the card sends RGB at 8 (NVIDIA)*, issues #4 and #17). Where the HDMI output would send HDR in RGB at only 8 bits (GeForce GTX 10, GTX 16 and RTX 20 cards, the TITAN V, and newer cards at 3840x2160 and 50 or 60 Hz without an HDMI 2.1 link), HDR now goes out as YCbCr 4:2:2 at 12 bits (10 on the GeForce GTX 10 cards and the TITAN V) at the same link speed. If the TV does not lock onto it, that mode goes back to 8 bits by itself for the session. *RGB at 12 bits first (GeForce GTX 16, RTX 20)*, off by default, sends RGB at 12 bits instead where the mode fits, as this setting did on those cards before; some TVs lock onto it and show nothing. At 10 bits it was seen working on a GeForce GTX 1060 with an LG OLED65C8 (issue #17).
- **HDMI variable refresh rate is left off during native Dolby Vision** on NVIDIA GeForce GTX 16 and RTX 20 graphics, and on RTX 30 cards whose firmware leaves the slot of the HDR10+ packet free (not on the RTX 40 and RTX 50 series: 1.1 does it on every card), whose driver turns it on in modes of 50 Hz or more whose rate reaches 110 % of the display's VRR minimum (with a minimum of 48 Hz: at 59.94 and 60 Hz, not at 50; issue #4). The kernel log says each change of the VRR packet.
- **What another program left on the output is put back** (LibreELEC forum thread 30675). After a Flatpak app that ran its own Wayland session (VacuumTube, for one), HDR10 came out as RGB at 8 bits, because the depth of the link that the other program had lowered stayed. On the first commit of Kodi on an output, `max bpc` goes back to the top of its range on amdgpu, i915 and xe, and a colour transform or lookup table left on the output is cleared. A clean output goes out as before.
- **Colour format and maximum bit depth** (*System > Display*, Expert, Automatic by default). They set the `color format` property that Linux 7.3 gives amdgpu, `NV_CONNECTOR_COLOR_FORMAT` of NVIDIA's driver 620, and `max bpc`. A format or depth that the driver cannot carry in a mode goes out as Automatic in that mode, and the log says so. After an idea of CroqueMr's fork (Kodi pull requests 30099 and 30192, by smp79).
- **HDR over HDMI on Intel HD and UHD 6xx graphics, as an expert choice** (*System > Display > HDR over HDMI on this Intel graphics*, shown only on that graphics). Its HDMI port has no packet of its own for HDR, so HDR10 and HLG went to a TV without Dolby Vision as SDR. On, the HDR data go out in another packet of the graphics chip. Off by default: nobody has tried it on a TV yet. The idea of that packet is CroqueMr's.
- **HDR10 whose mastering display is written in two ways no longer stutters.** The same mastering display comes as fractions in the HEVC data of a picture and as floats from Matroska. A stream whose pictures carried it one way and then the other made Kodi set up the output again at each change: a stutter, the display was told the data again, and native Dolby Vision ended and began again in the middle of the video. The values are now compared as HDMI codes them. A stream that carries the data only with its first picture keeps it after every key picture and after a seek. After an idea of CroqueMr's build.
- **A video whose container calls it SDR but whose stream says HLG or PQ** is taken as HDR from the start, so that its mode, its HDR signal and HLG sent as Dolby Vision are decided from the start, not from its first picture.
- **The check of which video the graphics can show follows the layout of each picture.** At start Kodi tries which video formats it can take from the hardware decoder. That test left out the memory layout that the driver gives each picture, which showing a picture uses, so on Intel and AMD graphics whose driver uses another layout Kodi could be left without its hardware renderer of video, or without 10-bit video in it. The test and the showing of a picture now describe each layer the same way. After an idea of CroqueMr's build.
- **Monitors on the same graphics card as the TV are no longer left on with a black picture** where Hades is not sure which output carries the sound. Only the output with the picture is kept; a display with no audio descriptors in its EDID cannot carry sound at all. If your monitors declare sound, this may not be enough: the `kodi.log` lines "stays on for" say why.
- **Only the display in use is lit, and the sound stays with it.** With several displays on one graphics card, the display whose number was chosen as the audio output was kept on, lit again after each video with the notification "The sound of ... comes back", and took the sound for a moment each time. The NVIDIA driver numbers its HDMI audio outputs by the order in which the displays came, so the number chosen in *System > Audio* can be another display from one start to the next. Now only the display that shows the picture is lit, and the sound goes to its own audio output (and from there to a sound bar or receiver on its eARC). The audio output is remembered by the display it was chosen with, not by its number; the setting itself is not changed, and `kodi.log` says where the sound went. For a receiver or sound bar on its own output of the graphics card, *System > Audio > Keep the display of the audio output on* (Expert, off by default) keeps that output on for its sound, as before.
- **Each output is matched with its own display.** Linux gives the EDID of a display a new number each time it reads it, and reuses old numbers, so Kodi could read another display's EDID for an output: with three displays on an NVIDIA card, the TV was taken for a monitor and a monitor for the TV, which decided the output kept on for the sound and the name a display is shown by. Kodi now reads each output's EDID as it is at that moment.
- **HDMI-CEC over a DisplayPort connector** (the AUX channel and the Linux CEC framework, with libcec), and **HDMI-CEC of SECO boards** (UDOO x86 and the like) as a module, which loads only on those boards.
- **Hades waits for the NVIDIA driver before it starts.** Linux loads the driver of an NVIDIA card while the rest of the system starts. Where Hades looked for the graphics before the driver was ready (the first start after an installation, or with the wait for the network turned off in the LibreELEC settings), it showed itself on no output, or on other graphics, until it was started again. It now waits for the driver, 20 seconds at most, and not at all on a PC without an NVIDIA card; the kernel log says how long it waited. Idea from CroqueMr's build.
- **A frame that the graphics driver refuses no longer leaves a half-drawn picture.** After three refused frames in a row, the picture that the screen still showed was drawn over. The buffer of a refused frame is now given back at once. After an idea of CroqueMr's build. Tested on the host.
- **Intel HDMI: the check of a TV after it drops its signal for a moment is no longer lost.** For 4 seconds after an HDMI hotplug, Hades looks at whether the TV still has the settings of the link, and sets the link up again where it lost them (an LG G5 does, after a Dolby Vision or refresh rate change). A look that came while a commit was under way was skipped without a word, which an HDR10+ video makes likely; it now counts as no look, for 10 more seconds at most, and the kernel log says when it gave up. After an idea of CroqueMr's build.
- **Intel Panther Lake and Wildcat Lake: the fallback for reading the EDID works.** Where the hardware transfer of the display's data channel fails, Linux reads it bit by bit, and its workaround for these chips did not work. Five fixes by Ville Syrjälä from the development tree of Intel's graphics, not in Linux mainline yet.
- **The kernel log says each InfoFrame of i915 and each packet of amdgpu** that goes out, when it changes, at most once a minute for those that change with each scene.
- **NVIDIA driver 620.70.06.** Its patches were remade for 620; 580.196.07 stays for the GeForce GTX 900 and GTX 10 cards and the TITAN V. With driver 580, HDR of more than 8 bits that Kodi draws into its own buffers is flipped on the graphics chip where the plane cannot reflect it (issue #17). The kernel log says what the EDID that the driver read holds. The installer entry no longer loads an NVIDIA module, so the console stays on the framebuffer of the boot.

**Picture and sound in step**

- **Picture in step with its sound** (*Settings > Player > Videos > Picture in step with its sound*, Advanced, on). On every driver the picture left the PC one refresh before its sound: 42 ms at 24 Hz and 17 ms at 60 Hz, as the logs of seven computers show. Hades now measures when each frame really reaches the screen and shows it with its sound. Which frames are seen, and for how long each, does not change: each one only starts at the right refresh. If you had set *Audio offset* to make up for this, set it back to 0. The offset of the frames from the refreshes is now also averaged on one branch: where a frame comes every second or fifth refresh (25 fps at 50 Hz, 23.976 fps at 119.88 Hz), its samples were a whole period apart. The averaging follows a finding of SamuriHL. Tested on the host. The idea of measuring the time to the screen is CorentinB's.
- **A pause keeps passthrough sound** (Dolby TrueHD, Dolby Digital Plus, Dolby Digital, DTS). A pause threw away the sound that Kodi had sent to the output and not yet played (up to 480 ms of TrueHD, about 200 ms of the others) and started the sync over at the resume: the sound came back further on than where it stopped, after a run of corrections, and while paused the receiver got silence, which it takes for the end of the stream. The sound now goes on where it stopped, in sync, with nothing lost or repeated, and while paused the receiver gets pause bursts that keep it on the stream. What the output already held when you paused still plays out with the picture stopped, as before. Measured on the host.
- **TrueHD comes back sooner after a start or a seek, and its corrections end where they should.** The measure of the sync at each start and seek turned all the TrueHD that the player had cached into silence, 680 ms, against about 300 ms of the other formats; TrueHD now comes back at about 320 ms. A correction of TrueHD stopped at less than half of the error; packets left out to catch up were counted as sound for up to 400 ms; and a correction longer than its pool of pauses let the sound through in the middle of it, with cuts. Each is fixed. After findings of SamuriHL and pannal. Measured on the host.
- **DTS-HD and TrueHD passthrough that could fail or crash.** A DTS-HD Master Audio stream at its peak bit rate could be taken for DTS-HD High Resolution after a few frames and crash Kodi; a burst of the largest size could not be sent; a DTS frame of an odd size sent one byte too many; a DTS-HD stream whose core changed size kept the timing of the first frames; and a DTS-HD frame that claims to be larger than the parser made passthrough stop for the rest of the stream. After a seek, a TrueHD stream could be taken for Dolby Digital Plus for a moment and the output opened twice. The first and the last are fixes still under review for Kodi, by tanio99 (for OSMC) and by heit88, taken with their authors. Tested on the host.
- **The sound output is closed without playing out its own silence** when a video starts and the format changes, and a resampler that cannot be set up no longer crashes Kodi: the audio engine opens its output again.

**Performance**

- **A lighter interface during native Dolby Vision, HDR10 and HLG.** Where no control changed, the pass that draws the interface is left out and the interface of the frame before is put over the picture, and only over the rectangles where the interface is, instead of the whole 3840x2160 frame. The same bytes go to the display, and a subtitle still goes out with its picture. `System.FPS` still counts such frames. A step of the conversion under the interface that saves nothing is left.
- **Dolby Vision made from HDR10, HDR10+ and profile 8.1 takes about a third less GPU time.** Their metadata map the picture to itself, so the decoder's picture now goes straight into the signal, the same bytes: an HDR10+ picture sent as Dolby Vision took 35.0 ms instead of 53.5 on a software renderer. Where small graphics cannot keep up, frames with the player information or menus over such a picture go out converted while they are on screen, only where measured to help; frames without them are unchanged.
- **Dolby Vision packing takes less GPU time while the interface is over the picture** (menus, the player information, subtitles), with the same bytes. The value that the two pixels of a pair share is worked out once, through the quad operations of the GPU, instead of twice, and black or opaque pixels of the interface take a shorter path. Measured on the host with two of Mesa's software renderers, with the interface over part of the picture: 14 to 28 % less with zink and 6 to 11 % less with llvmpipe (3 to 4 % where the interface is composited first). Without the interface it is unchanged. After ideas of CroqueMr's build.
- **No pause where a video mapped to the display starts.** The colour table that a mapping to the display needs (Low-Latency, Dolby Vision or HDR10 mapped to an HDR10 display, anything mapped to SDR) was made when its first picture was drawn, on the thread of the interface: 0.64 s of one processor core of a Ryzen 9 7950X3D. It is now made ahead, on a thread of its own, as soon as the video is set up.
- **Frames ahead within seconds where a graphics chip cannot keep up at all.** A GT 1030 with a profile 7 film missed up to half of its frames, and the old test took 48 seconds to see it.
- **Less memory for the HEVC decoder.** The pool of video surfaces is sized from the level of the stream (a 4K level 5 stream: 14 surfaces instead of 22, 20 instead of 28 with two layers), and with nvidia-vaapi-driver the surface given back last goes out first, so that only as many surfaces get an image as are held at once. Meant for graphics cards with 2 GB.
- **With nvidia-vaapi-driver, a surface of the enhancement layer that was let go without being drawn is synced when it is taken again.** That driver copies a decoded picture on a thread of its own, and a copy that ended late could mark the next picture as copied.
- **Smaller things:** a video paused in its last seconds no longer keeps a processor core busy (71 % of a core before, 2.3 % now); the debug information on screen takes its figures twice a second instead of in every frame, so it no longer redraws the interface over HDR; thumbnails of HDR videos take 98 MB at most instead of 163 MB, and thumbnails of DVDs and HDR videos no longer set their colour conversion up again for each one; a picture shown at its own size is never sent through a high quality scaler (it was with the threshold at 0 %).

**Discs and files**

- **Films on DVD and in interlaced video play as the film** (*Settings > Player > Videos > Rebuild films from interlaced video*, Advanced, on). A film stored as interlaced video (3:2 pulldown on NTSC discs and broadcasts, a PAL film marked interlaced, also one field off, and a film at 25 in 29.97 progressive frames) was deinterlaced at 59.94 or 50 Hz, which softens fine detail and makes motion uneven. Before a DVD title or an MPEG-2 file plays, Hades now decodes a few seconds of its start and of three places further on; where they all show the same film, each picture is put back together from its own fields, the repeated ones are left out, and the film plays at its own rate (23.976, 25 or 29.97 Hz). Video that is really interlaced, titles that mix film and video and anything Hades is not sure of play as before, and a frame whose fields do not match (a cut) is deinterlaced on its own. Measured on made-up clips, every picture is the film's, field for field, and no clip of video is taken for film. The idea is CorentinB's.
- **Films with soft telecine play at 23.976 Hz, each picture once.** An MPEG-2 film (a DVD, or a file made from one) is often coded at 23.976 pictures per second with flags that show it at 29.97, and Kodi took it for 29.97, chose 59.94 Hz and showed pictures in uneven steps. It is now told before the first picture, from the flags of the stream and without decoding: in a file by what most of it is (an intro in video before the film does not decide), and in a DVD image (ISO) or `VIDEO_TS` folder title by title, read by a reader of its own (not on a disc in a drive). The repeated field of a picture now lengthens it and no longer delays its start. A film at 24 in such a stream is shown at 24 Hz.
- **Streaming: a connection that drops no longer ends the film.** When the network or a streaming service stopped sending for a while, the video ended as if it had been watched to the end (and an add-on could mark it as watched and start the next episode). Hades now buffers, with the picture and the clock paused together, says "Connection interrupted" after 5 seconds, reconnects at the byte it had reached and plays on from where it stopped, in sync. After 5 minutes without data, or when the server refuses the link (an expired one), playback stops with an error and keeps its position. A real end of the file is unchanged. After ideas of malard and pannal for Kodi. Tried in a virtual machine.
- **A video asked for while another is still opening no longer waits for it,** and is no longer lost when the first one fails to open (issue #21). Stopping a stream whose server does not answer no longer freezes the interface for a minute, a video on a server of the home network no longer waits while Kodi reads a whole file looking for subtitles beside it, and a read over the network gives what it has at once instead of waiting for more. Tried in a virtual machine.
- **A file that stops answering no longer freezes the interface** (issue #21: a wait of one to two minutes when opening a film on an NTFS disk, and a film that stopped after two minutes). The reads of the background scan of a folder, and of the thumbnail and the probe of a file, can be stopped from another thread; stopping the loader says what it waits for and the interface keeps drawing; and a read that ends because nothing came for 20 seconds says so, with the file and the byte, instead of looking like the end of the file. The cause was the new `ntfs` driver of Linux 7.3, fixed below.
- **A folder that cannot be read to its end is reported,** not listed short, with the folder, the number of entries read and the error.
- **The library knows the Dolby Vision profile and the HDR of what you play, also of a `.strm`, a disc or a stream.** Kodi reads the HDR of a file when it scans it; a `.strm` (of a streaming add-on, or one that names a file), a Blu-ray folder or image and a stream only got what the player knew as it began. When a video ends, what its playback found now goes into its library details, as a scan writes them: the profile (with FEL or MEL for profile 7), the HDR10+ or HLG of a Dolby Vision video, and HDR10+. Nothing that the details already say is changed, nor details from an NFO. A `.strm` that names a file of this system or of the home network is read when the library is scanned, as that file would be. The HDR type of a picture also no longer comes from the picture before it (an HDR10 video after a Dolby Vision one with HDR10+ was taken as HDR10+). Tried in a virtual machine with a test add-on.
- **Profile 7 files played from discs and streams keep "7FEL" or "7MEL"** in the details that Kodi stores, as a library scan writes them, instead of "7".
- **Blu-ray: the audio or subtitle stream that you choose is the one that plays,** with the disc's menus and without them, and the disc is told, so that the next play items keep it. The picture-in-picture video and the secondary audio of a disc are never opened in place of the film. The audio and subtitles that a disc chooses before its playlist is ready are given again once the play item is known (issue #1: the subtitles chosen in a menu were not on when the film started), and the log says each choice.
- **Blu-ray: a play item that starts between key pictures plays from its first picture, with its Dolby Vision enhancement layer.** Where a play item (the next part of a playlist, or where a menu jumps to) starts at a picture that is not a key picture, the pictures before it are now decoded and not shown, also with the DRM PRIME decoder, so it plays from its start instead of up to a second later. After a jump or a seek in a film with a full enhancement layer, the layer is decoded from the first picture (from about the 22nd before, in the test disc), and the look for it reads the disc in runs instead of one small piece at a time. With and without the disc's menus. After an idea of CroqueMr's build.
- **Blu-ray: chapter marks, joins and subtitles around a jump.** After a seek, the marks that share a packet of the disc with its target still come. With the disc's menus, the first bytes of a play item whose streams are opened again are no longer read by the play item before it (its video started 0.125 s late), and the subtitles before its start are not shown. Played without its menus, a join that is not seamless no longer loses half a second of video, nor shifts the timeline. A seek that came right after the reader passed the end time of a play item no longer skips the rest of it. The length, chapters and chapter seeks stay after the end of a playlist, and a seek no longer loses its key picture to a packet read before it (two fixes by matthane in Kodi's `master`). The lost marks were found by CroqueMr, and telling a play item before its bytes is his idea.
- **Blu-ray menus.** The menus of an Ultra HD Blu-ray whose film is HDR10 or Dolby Vision are made for that picture, and they were drawn as if they were SDR: grey and washed out. They are now drawn as the disc made them, also when its Java application draws them. The colours of the menus of an HD disc are read in the colour matrix of its video. A Java menu that animates without a pause is now shown while it moves, a subtitle, audio or angle that a Java disc sets before its playlist starts is kept, and the fully transparent picture that many Java discs keep over the film is no longer drawn over every frame. The arrows and seeking during the film go to Kodi also on the Java discs that keep their menu graphics open. After findings of SamuriHL and pannal.
- **Blu-ray: VC-1 at its own rate.** A VC-1 video of a Blu-ray, and of a `.m2ts` or `.ts` file played on its own, opened at twice its frame rate (47.952 for 23.976) and could change the mode of the display twice; it now opens at the rate it declares. After an idea of SamuriHL.
- **A disc image over the network opens with its menu, and faster.** A Blu-ray image played over HTTP (a `.strm` that names an image on a cloud drive) did not open with its menu: Kodi took a short read from the network for an error. Such an image is now also read in pieces of 64 KiB with the last ones kept, which halves the requests: its menu opened in 6.7 s instead of 13.0, at 100 ms of network latency in a virtual machine.
- **A seek step is no longer lost** when it is pressed while the picture of the step before it is still coming (the second "-1 minute" of two); it is made once the picture is there.
- **Picture subtitles.** A picture subtitle (PGS, DVB, DVD) that Matroska keeps a millisecond after its frame now shows with that frame, not a frame late or never, and a PGS end segment without its display set no longer draws the subtitle again (fixes after matthane's for CoreELEC). The subtitles of a Dolby Vision profile 5 video are drawn as HDR, as the video is. Two new settings, in *Settings > Player > Subtitles*: *Bitmap subtitle saturation* makes their colours paler, if they are too vivid for you (100 % leaves them as they were made), and *Bitmap subtitles made for* tells subtitles made for SDR in an HDR video, and the other way round (Automatic by default, which in an HDR video draws as SDR only subtitles brighter than any made for HDR). After ideas of avdvplus, pannal and CroqueMr.
- **3D: side by side at 1080p for a 3D display that takes it without listing it;** after an idea of cinemaONE. **3D over a passive DisplayPort adaptor** (DP++) that says HDMI, on AMD and NVIDIA graphics; Intel already sent it; the idea is cinemaONE's. On AMD, HDR10 through such an adaptor reached the display as no HDR at all, a fault of Linux that is fixed.
- **3D: the lines between the two eyes of frame packing are black.** They kept whatever the buffer had, and the display was sent it. After an idea of CroqueMr's build.
- **3D Blu-ray: the second view after a picture that empties the decoder (an IDR).** In two test streams, 11 of 50 pictures of the second view differed from those of the reference decoder of the standard; they are now the same.
- **A Matroska seek starts at the earliest key frame of the video tracks,** so that Dolby Vision in two video tracks keeps its enhancement layer after a seek.

**Sound and hardware**

- **Keeping an NVIDIA card at full speed while HD audio plays through it** (*Keep the graphics card at full speed while HD audio plays through*, Advanced, off by default, issue #8). The sound of TrueHD or DTS-HD cut out every few seconds on a laptop on its charger, because the card changed speed and the DMA of its audio function lost samples. It follows the sound to another NVIDIA card.
- **HDMI-CEC:** the keys of the remote work sooner after a start where an amplifier answers slowly, since Hades asks it only what volume control needs; and six faults of libCEC that freed memory while its threads still used it, which could crash Kodi when a device stopped answering or the adapter went away, are fixed, and so is a seventh, where libCEC read the key held on the remote without its lock while it closed. Tested on the host with an emulated adapter.
- **Infrared remotes that need a decoder of their own** (`pulse_distance`, `pulse_length`, `manchester`, `rc_mm`, `raw`, `grundig`, `samsung36`, the Xbox DVD remote and the iMON RSC) get decoders compiled from source with the compiler of the build, in place of objects compiled in 2021; your keymaps and decoders in `/storage/.config/rc_keymaps/protocols` work as before. With today's compiler the `manchester` decoder would not have loaded, which is fixed. Checked on a virtual receiver.
- **A disk in a USB enclosure is stopped when the system powers off,** with its heads parked, instead of losing its power while it spins. Disks of removable media are left alone. After an idea of Pro-me3us for CoreELEC.
- **Ryzen PCs that only sleep to idle reach their deepest sleep** (the AMD platform driver); **the ALSA loopback card** for routing sound into a room-correction program such as CamillaDSP; and **btproxy**, BlueZ's tool that gives a virtual machine the Bluetooth controller of its host (asked for by lfemperor).
- **The kernel log says the PCIe link at each stream DMA error** of the HD audio core.
- **The fast side of an external USB hub may sleep when nothing is on it.** A setting inherited from 2014 kept them awake. The diagnostics list the hubs.

**Network, remote control apps and add-ons**

- **JSON-RPC serves each connection on its own** (Kodi's change by Martin Ellis, with a test by Garrett Brown): a dialog that one request opens no longer holds up every other remote control app and notification. Hades adds five fixes of its own to that server: a notification no longer closes its connection, a client that closes its sending side still gets its answers, a connection that the server cannot watch is refused instead of overwriting memory, the server no longer spins when no descriptor is left, and what it sends to a WebSocket client (Home Assistant uses one) is freed. Tested on the host.
- **Samba shares a drive whose label holds `%`, a backslash or brackets** under a safe name: such a label could add lines to the configuration of Samba, a share of the whole system among them. Tested inside the image.
- **For skins and add-ons:** an animated image plays from its first frame each time it shows, and `Window(Home).Property(MousePointerStays)` lets a remote that drives the pointer keep it on screen when a key is pressed (after ideas of YBLOD).

**System**

- **Linux 7.3.4,** with 41 fixes to `fs/ntfs` from mainline that it does not have, one that makes a listing with a damaged index report an error and one that stops a write cut by a full disk from leaving the file longer; the Wi-Fi fixes of `mt7921` and `mt7925` that keep the join channel until the station is authorized; two fixes for Intel Wildcat Lake graphics; and AMD's fix from Linux 7.4 for the display of DCN 4.2B processors, which used a cache that they do not have. The patches keep their authors.
- **Up to date:** LibreELEC `master` up to 23b6180439a3 (Mesa 26.3.0, zlib 1.4 with its fixes for CVE-2026-85091 and CVE-2026-4738, OpenSSH 10.6p2, BlueZ 5.88, systemd 262.2, OpenVPN 2.7.9, HarfBuzz 15.1.0, 7-Zip 26.06, libplist 2.8.1, Intel's processor microcode of 2026-11-10 and exfatprogs 1.4.4), Kodi 22.0rc2 of the `Piers` branch with its later commits up to 169296d6bd84, and libplacebo d9ab3a48a55a. The patches of Hades that these versions contain were removed.
- **Devices reach only their own memory in the normal start.** The boot entries Run and Live now start the kernel with DMA translation (`iommu.passthrough=0 intel_iommu=on,igfx_off`): where the PC has an IOMMU, a network or Wi-Fi card, a USB controller or the graphics card can use only the memory that the kernel gives it, so a device whose firmware was replaced cannot read the rest. On AMD this is what Ubuntu, Fedora, Debian and Arch do by default; on Intel, the integrated graphics are left out, as Debian does. "Run (internal disks read-only)" starts as before and is the way back if a device misbehaves. An update in place adds the words to the menus once. Tried in virtual machines with the IOMMU of Intel and of AMD.
- **The diagnostics:** they record the video memory of an NVIDIA card during Dolby Vision, and now the memory of an AMD card too, also an integrated one; how long the start took and what it waited for; and what the IOMMU does with the memory of devices. They copy each answer of the graphics cards as soon as it comes, and replace the name of the system, wherever it appears as a word of its own, with a marker, so that a diagnostics folder does not carry the name of your PC. The filter, also that of Submit Log, now takes the serial numbers of FAT and NTFS volumes, also as GRUB's menu names them, the labels and partition names that `lsblk` and `parted` list, the share of a drive in Samba's configuration, the folder of a Kodi profile, the serial numbers that `lsusb -v`, `lspci -vvv` and the list of input devices print, the names of networks in the list of `connmanctl services`, and the name of the system after the times of every format of the journal. Folders that an earlier version wrote are filtered again at the next start with the switch on. While the diagnostics are on, their query of an NVIDIA card runs at the lowest priority.
- **The share Logfiles gives the log through the filter.** It gave LibreELEC's log zip as the system wrote it, with the keys of add-ons, the names of Wi-Fi networks and serial numbers in it. `createlog` over SSH still makes the unfiltered zip, as in LibreELEC; the share does not show it.
- **The diagnostics keep a start of the Installer entry too.** That start keeps nothing of its own (its storage is in RAM). With the diagnostic switch on, its journal and the installer's log go to `hades-diag` on the drive as it runs, every two seconds, so that an installer that hangs or shows nothing leaves a record (8 MB and 1 MB at most; the drive is made read-only again after each write, so turning the PC off by force leaves it clean). Idea from CroqueMr's build. Tested in a virtual machine turned off by force.
- **The Windows installer has its own look:** an icon, a side bar that shows the steps, light and dark following Windows, the serial number of the drive always visible, a window that shows each stage while it works, and a last page that says how to start Hades and what each entry of the boot menu does. Disabled controls are readable in both themes. An update keeps the wait of the boot menu and the display options that the menus hold. Tested on a virtual disk.
- **The same commit builds the same system.** Every date, the build identity and the order of the release tar now come from the time of the commit, and packages no longer pick up the version of the build tree. Two assemblies of the image gave the same system, kernel and release tar byte for byte; the image differs only in the identifiers of its partitions and file systems, which are random by design, and in the key that signs the kernel modules, which is made new for each build.
- **Tests in the tree.** `tools/regress` now carries host tests of the build: an independent check of the Dolby Vision composer of ETSI GS CCM 001, the tests of the 3D Blu-ray decoder, a smoke test of the interface in a virtual machine, a check of the strings of the skin, the black and the colour of the Dolby Vision signal measured against the standards, the shares of Samba, and others.
- **Updates** hides the entries that have nothing to act on while Hades has no update server, and *Available Versions* says how to install a new version also with *Automatic Updates* on "auto".

### Details

**Dolby Vision**

- The default transport (`videoscreen.hadesdvtransport`) is "As composed" (2): the composer's 12-bit YCbCr of profiles 7 and 8, as it is; "As licensed players send (IPT)" (0) is the IPT-PQ-c2 of before, and a value that cannot be read gives the default. The bridge's object code is the same. Measured with `tools/regress/chroma` against a full 4:4:4 picture in delta E ITP (ITU-R BT.2124), for a display that rebuilds the right pixel of a pair with a four-tap filter, on pictures of two profile 7 films: a mean of 0.127 and 0.131 as composed against 0.163 and 0.178 in IPT, and a largest error of 6.5 and 6.7 against 6.5 and 17.8. The bytes of the IPT model are the bridge's to within one code in 99.96 % of samples.
- The check of the reshaping curve (`dvbridge_map_color()`) accepts repeated pivots, with the piece of a sample picked by the first pivot above it (ETSI GS CCM 001, 5.4.2.2 and 5.4.2.3). libplacebo does the same (`libplacebo-9979`): its reshaping picks the piece by the sample as it is, so that a pivot that equals the one before it behaves as the specification says. A frame that is not drawn keeps a fixed text for each check (`dvbridge_render_not_drawn()`) beside why the metadata were refused (`dvbridge_render_refused()`).
- The trim pass follows ETSI TS 103 572 (4.3, equations 4 to 8: slope, offset, power, chroma weight and saturation gain, 2048 when absent) and SMPTE ST 2094-10:2021 (Annex B.3, equations 8 and 9, an informative example in linear light), for the level 2 block whose target peak equals the peak of the display to within one code, on Hades' own curve. It is drawn only with `videoscreen.hadesdvtrimpass` on, and never with `videoscreen.hadesdvnotrims` on, which still decides alone what native output sends. Against a colourist's own 1000-nit grade of an open test film (CC BY 4.0), six frames: the mean delta E ITP went from 25.3 without the trims to 47.2 with them, and under 100 cd/m² from 18.1 to 53.0. There is no interpolation between blocks, because the standard does not define one, and no level 8, whose algorithm is not public.
- HLG sent as Dolby Vision is profile 8.1 over a PQ picture that libplacebo makes of the HLG picture, not a profile 8.4 with a made-up reshaping, which would only approximate the OOTF of BT.2100. With the setting off, the bytes sent are the same as before.
- The update of libplacebo leaves what native Dolby Vision sends byte for byte as it was. It changes two conversions of HLG to a 4000 cd/m² target, in values of 0.06 or less, because libplacebo dropped a floor that BT.2100 does not have.
- The output that native output takes is held by `COutputHandover` (`xbmc/windowing/gbm/drm/OutputHandover.cpp`), with its decisions in `OutputState.h` and its calls to libdrm in `LibdrmKms.h`. It captures every property of the connector, the CRTC and the plane in use that is not read-only (a blob by its bytes), except those that something else owns, that cannot be put back or that are not a state, each named with its reason. What the transport sets is of two kinds: its signals (the Dolby Vision, Low-Latency, HDMI 1.4b and HDR tunnel flags, and the InfoFrame of nvidia-drm), which every attempt carries, and the settings that it makes neutral (depth, range, colorimetry, colour format, HDR metadata, dithering, HDMI VRR, colour processing), which a refusal can leave at the transport's value, with a warning in the log. The end puts in its request only what is not as it was found, reads the output back once the commit is taken, and sets again in a commit of its own what is not as found. Objects are told by their ids. What the transport made of the output is kept in `/run/hades-dv-state`, written whole with a checksum. `DetectedProperty.h` keeps what the kernel said of the converter behind a port, and of a dual-mode port of nvidia-drm, until the display is looked at again, instead of reading the kernel with each picture. These files are written from the KMS interface and are GPL-2.0-or-later.
- With driver 620, the transport sets `NV_CONNECTOR_OUTPUT_BPC` and `NV_CONNECTOR_COLOR_FORMAT` to `auto` where the connector has them, for Standard, Low-Latency and the HDMI 1.4b signalling (`TransportSpecs.h`), and `nvidia-open-kernel-modules-9062` refuses a connector state with Dolby Vision unless both are `auto`, 8 or RGB.
- `linux-9993` offers the tunnel on DCN 4.2 and 4.2B, reviewed stage by stage in 7.3.4, with a check of the validated context and a read back of 90 fields of the pipe each time the tunnel goes on; `linux-9994` refuses from a failed read back on every commit whose stream carries the tunnel (DCE 11.2 and 12 too), and Kodi leaves native output after 24 refused frames or 2 s, as it does for any refusal. `linux-9992` is AMD's `60484b2f024b` of Linux 7.4 (Gabe Teeger).
- A passive DP++ adaptor: `linux-9995` (i915, the tunnel through an adaptor that identifies itself as HDMI; a level shifter on a native HDMI port is not a conversion, as before), `linux-9998` (amdgpu, the tunnel and its properties on such a DisplayPort connector, with `DVBRIDGE_DUAL_MODE`), `linux-9996` (amdgpu, HDMI 3D through it), `linux-9997` (amdgpu, the HDR InfoFrame laid out for the link that carries it, not for the connector: through such an adaptor it went out as a DisplayPort SDP on an HDMI link) and `linux-9999` (KUnit, 7 cases). `nvidia-open-kernel-modules-9071` and `nvidia-legacy-open-9076`: frame packing on a dual-mode DisplayPort connector, where NVKMS drives a TMDS output as HDMI. The clock limit of the adaptor (type 1: 165 MHz, type 2: its register) is kept by the drivers.
- The EDID: `nvidia-open-kernel-modules-9070` and `nvidia-legacy-open-9075` keep, in the bytes that NVKMS hands on, the blocks that an HDMI Forum EEODB counts beyond the extension count of a base block of version 1.4 (NVKMS's own parse is unchanged); `linux-9991` keeps the valid blocks that an HF-EEODB counts when one of them is not, instead of falling back to the base count. In Kodi, `DV_DISPLAY::Reason()` tells no Dolby Vision block from an EDID read in part, and the log line "DVBridge: the display's EDID, as native output reads it" names the missing blocks, the version and interface of each Dolby Vision block, and the other vendor video blocks.
- The Radeon setting: `linux-9989` gives the HDMI connectors of DCE 11.2 to 12.1 (`amdgpu.dvbridge_dce`) and of Intel display version 9 (`i915.hdmi_hdr_gmp`) an immutable property, `HADES_BOOT_OPTION`, that names the parameter; Kodi shows the setting only there and writes the value into both boot menus (`internal-disks option`, with the lock and the written-through copy of the other boot settings), where `i915.hdmi_hdr_gmp=0 amdgpu.dvbridge_dce=0` now stand on Run, internal and Live. With `amdgpu.dvbridge_dce=1`, the capture about 15 s into native Dolby Vision writes `crc.txt`: the CRC of the active CRTC of each amdgpu card for 12 frames.
- "Check the Dolby Vision output" (`LibreELEC-settings-9032`, `hades-output-check` of hades-diag): before Kodi starts, it shows test frames of the tunnel and reads the card's CRC of its output (`crtc-N/crc/data` in debugfs), and judges by linear relations over GF(2) between the CRCs of frames that differ in known bits, calibrated first, instead of an expected CRC; a dither that changes in time, a conversion and a truncation each give their own answer. Tested with `vkms`, the virtual display of the kernel.
- HDR10+ and HDR10 sent as Dolby Vision, and profile 8.1 made from HDR10, map each component to the composer with the identity; for them the source transport now takes the decoder's planes as the composer's (ETSI GS CCM 001, 5.4: each sample s composes to 4 s), the same bytes. Frames with GUI elements over a picture in YCbCr without an enhancement residual have a converted level 1 that `CDVGuiLevel` uses only where it measures a saving (on a software renderer, 21 % less with the player information over half of the picture; nothing with subtitles alone).
- `kodi-9952` and `kodi-9953`: where fragment shaders have the quad operations of `GL_KHR_shader_subgroup`, pass 4 of the source transport (`PACK`, and `PACK_GUI` where the interface is read in place) gives the odd pixel of a pair its even neighbour's two chroma codes, and whether anything is drawn over it, through `subgroupQuadSwapHorizontal()`, tagged with the raster position they are of; the packer that reads the interface in place keeps each quad together until the swap. A black texel of the interface takes its PQ value from the texel itself, once for the three components, and the picture under an opaque one is not read. The program as written stays the fallback wherever the quad form does not compile or link, and the reference of a check of the bytes packs without quad operations. Packed 3840x2160 frames of a profile 7 FEL stream, its base layer alone and HDR10+ sent as Dolby Vision, with interfaces of every kind, are identical to before in 18 cases of 18 on llvmpipe and zink. GPU time of the packing, before and after, with the interface over half of the picture read in place: 93.5 to 80.7 ms (FEL) and 112.5 to 81.1 ms (HDR10+ as Dolby Vision) on zink, 87.6 to 78.5 and 98.4 to 92.4 ms on llvmpipe; composited first, 59.2 to 56.9 and 45.6 to 44.1 ms; nothing drawn over, 18.0 and 17.4 ms on zink.
- Two video streams of one picture (one with a Dolby Vision record, one with HDR10+ only) are paired by `CDVDDemuxFFmpeg::ChooseHdrStream()`, which opens the one that `videoscreen.hadesdualhdr` and the display choose. Two whole tracks that the container groups as Dolby Vision layers are said as such in the log, not as a lone base layer.

**HDR and the signal to the TV**

- The BT.2390 curve of the bridge takes the knee of ITU-R BT.2390, KS = 1.5 maxLum - 0.5 (Report ITU-R BT.2408-9 Annex 5, step 2, to which BT.2390-12 now refers), instead of libplacebo's default knee offset of 1.0 (KS = 2 maxLum - 1). On a neutral ramp (masters of 1000, 4000 and 10000 cd/m², SDR whites of 100, 203 and 300, HDR10 displays of 600 and 1400) the bridge is within 0.17 of a code of the EETF computed from the report's text (before: up to 153 codes away). On 16 pictures of two Dolby Vision profile 7 FEL films mapped to SDR at 203 cd/m², where the display can show the source: 0.82 delta E ITP from it on average instead of 3.12. The curve of before stays available as "BT.2390, libplacebo's knee" (a tone mapping method of the video, and a value of *Tone mapping curve*), off by default and byte for byte the same; it gives what is above the display's peak about 1.7 times more range (on real pictures 26 to 40 % more of its gradation) at the cost of darker middle tones. The black and the first codes of a picture mapped to a display are checked against BT.2390 (`tools/regress/black`).
- HDR10+ mapped by the bridge: `dvbridge_hdr10plus_map.h` maps a picture with its SMPTE ST 2094-40 metadata through libplacebo's `pl_tone_map_st2094_40`, with the studio's knee point and Bezier curve where the video carries them; the decoder holds each picture's metadata as it holds its InfoFrame (`CMetadataHold`: up to 11 pictures without their own keep the last ones, the 12th goes back to the static metadata, a seek keeps nothing). In Low-Latency, `dvbridge_render_set_hdr10plus()` gives the bridge the metadata of each picture of HDR10+ sent as Dolby Vision, and the RPU row of the player information says "L1 from HDR10+, its tone curve applied". The curve was checked against an independent implementation of the standard and the metadata of an open test film.
- `videoscreen.hadeshdrassdr` sends the chosen HDR videos down the path of a display without HDR, with the tone mapping "Reference (BT.2390)" for the display of `videoscreen.hadesdvsdrtarget`; it is greyed out while Kodi's "Adjust display HDR mode" is off. Where the shaders draw a picture of BT.2020 primaries in BT.709, the display is now told BT.709 (Kodi's own fix of issue 28468 covered PQ with tone mapping only). `libplacebo-9981` tunes the peak of an HLG picture only to a target of an HDR transfer. The custom SDR display is a white of 20 to 500 cd/m² and a pure power gamma of 1.8 to 2.8; the black of the display (0 to 0.5 cd/m²) is the black level lift of BT.2390 (5.4.1), in PQ after libplacebo's mapping, as Low-Latency has it.
- HDR at more than 8 bits on NVIDIA: `nvidia-open-kernel-modules-9063` gives driver 620 the parameter `hades_hdr_ycbcr422` of nvidia-modeset (the name of the one of driver 580): in a mode set that signals HDR where NVKMS chose RGB at 8 bpc over HDMI (no 10 bpc over HDMI on Turing, 2160p50 and 60 over TMDS without FRL, a display without deep colour), the mode is validated in YCbCr 4:2:2 at 12 bpc at the clock of RGB at 8 bpc first. Kodi decides from what NVKMS chose, reads the display's SCDC 2.5 s after the commit and, where the display does not lock, steps that mode down for the session (`HdrMoreBits.h`). `nvidia-open-kernel-modules-9064` says each change of the HDMI VRR packet; `nvidia-open-kernel-modules-9065` writes "HDMI VRR left off" only where NVKMS's own rule (`nvAdjustHwModeTimingsForVrrEvo()`) would have turned it on.
- HDR over the tunnel: `DVBRIDGE_HDR10_TUNNEL_LAB` (`linux-9973` to `9975`) on i915, xe and amdgpu sends HDR in the RGB 8 bpc tunnel as YCbCr 4:2:2 at 12 bits, with the AVI InfoFrame of 4:2:2, BT.2020 and limited range, the HDR static metadata of the stream and no Dolby vendor InfoFrame; on amdgpu the vendor slot is left free, as on i915 (`linux-9986`), and the scanout check takes the stream's own HDR packet with it (`linux-9982`). Kodi asks for it where `HDR10Tunnel.h` says so: the setting, a kernel backend with the property, HDMI over TMDS, an EDID with 4:2:2 and an HDR block, a picture that the bridge draws as it is (PQ or HLG, BT.2020, progressive, 2D), 3840x2160 at 50 or 60 Hz, nothing of the user's overridden, and an ordinary output that would send RGB at 8 bits or 4:2:0 (asked once for each mode with a TEST_ONLY commit). `HDR10TunnelGuard.h` gives the mode up for the session where more than 36 of 90 frames take more than 0.85 of the period on the GPU, or their average more than the period. Measured on the host against an independent reference: luma and chroma within one code of 4095 on real streams with the interface over them, and the black bars exact.
- `linux-9988`: on display version 9, with `i915.hdmi_hdr_gmp=1`, the HDR static metadata go out in the gamut metadata DIP of the transcoder, which these chips have, in place of the DRM InfoFrame DIP that version 10 added; the connector then gets `HDR_OUTPUT_METADATA`. Written from i915; not tried on a TV.
- `Player.Process(videooutputhdrtype)` and `videooutputeotf` come from the blob of `HDR_OUTPUT_METADATA` that the kernel holds for the id of the last accepted commit; the blob is read once for each id, also when the kernel gives a new blob an id that it used before.
- Sound and displays (`kodi-9961`): `ActiveAE::INTERNAL::OutputForDisplayInUse()` decides before `FollowedDisplayOutput()`, by the display each device setting was chosen with (`audiooutput.hadesdevicedisplay` and `audiooutput.hadespassthroughdisplay`: maker, product and name from the ELD, kept the first time the output names a display) and the display Kodi draws on; with `audiooutput.hadeslightforsound` off, `KEEP_OUTPUTS::OutputsForSound()` keeps no output on for any sound and logs every output that could carry sound with its display; the notification that an output comes back for its sound is shown once for each output in a run. `CDRMConnector::GetEDID()` (`kodi-9960`) reads the `EDID` property again and takes its blob only when the id held.
- The converter: `DVBRIDGE_DV_CONVERTER` (none, unlisted, listed, forced with `dv_pcon=1`; `linux-9976` to `9978`), the console without the tunnel on that connector (`linux-9983`), and a refusal said once at info level (`linux-9984`).
- `HdrStaticMetadata.h`: the luminances in steps of 0.0001 cd/m², the primaries and the white point in steps of 0.00002, both pictures with a luminance or neither; the content light levels are still not compared, as in Kodi. `CInBandHdrMetadata` keeps the last static metadata of a stream for each transfer function, in the FFmpeg decoder and in DRM PRIME. A HEVC stream whose VUI says PQ or HLG is taken with the colour that its decoder gives it, not the container's.
- `CVaapi2Texture`: one description of a layer for the probe and the import (`DescribeLayer()`, `ImportAttributes()`), with the modifier of its object where EGL takes modifiers, never `DRM_FORMAT_MOD_INVALID`, and a layout other than linear refused where EGL takes no modifiers, as the bridge decides.
- `nvidia-drm-wait.service` (`/usr/lib/nvidia/drm-wait`) waits for a DRM device under each NVIDIA display device of the PCI bus, and stops waiting once `nvidia-drm` is loaded without one for a device, once udev has had nothing to do for 2 s while it is not loaded, or after 20 s. Kodi is ordered after it; it loads nothing.
- `CDRMUtils::LastBufferTaken()` and `CGBMSurface::GiveBackRefused()`: the buffer of a commit that the driver refused, also the fallback to the request before it, is given back at once, so that the queue of locked buffers holds only buffers that are on the screen or on their way to it.
- `linux-9981`: a look at the SCDC that `intel_hdmi_reset_link()` put off because a commit had not reached `hw_done` is not counted among the looks of `linux-9934`. `linux-9852` to `9856`: commits `96cb6f956651`, `39c2d49c9ece`, `e860dbc18495`, `548a600780cc` and `b21a0ab8fb95` of drm-intel-next, for Wa_16025573575. `CONFIG_CEC_SECO=m`.
- `linux-9979` and `linux-9980`: the InfoFrames of i915 and the packets of amdgpu in the kernel log, nothing for a repeat, one line a minute for those that change with the scene, 400 lines in all; on DCE, only those of a commit that enables a stream, not those of a check (`linux-9985`).
- NVIDIA's 620 gives the HDMI connectors `NV_CONNECTOR_COLOR_FORMAT` (auto, RGB, YCbCr 4:2:2, YCbCr 4:4:4); 4:2:0 has no value there, and the log says so once.
- `linux-9987`: side by side (half) at 1080p, with the timings of 1080p, is added for a display that says 3D_present and does not list it, as drm_edid.c adds the modes that HDMI 1.4b makes mandatory.
- The display watch (`CDRMDisplayWatch`) moves Kodi to a display connected on the same card with `CWinSystemGbm::MoveOutputTo()` (the driver asked first, one commit, Kodi back where it was if the driver refuses), in 29 to 61 ms in a virtual machine against 4.8 to 5.8 s for a restart; another card, Kodi without an output or a refusal still restart Kodi. A display that takes the place of the one in use is read again at the hot-plug event (`RefreshModes()`).

**Picture and sound in step**

- `MeasuredLatency.h`: in each display mode the time from a flip to the screen is measured with the out-fence of KMS while the video plays, kept (`videoscreen.hadeslatency`) and used from the next video or skip; the render manager chooses each frame for that time instead of Kodi's fallback of three periods. The A/V line of the log now takes the sound where the audio engine measured it. `CVsyncPhase` unwraps each offset of a frame from the refreshes towards a running reference before it is averaged.
- Passthrough pause (`ActiveAESink`, `ActiveAE`): a pause hands the packets that the sink has not written back to the engine, marked unwritten and in order (no burst is cut); while paused the sink sends IEC 61937 pause bursts; the resume puts them out first, with the sync held, and its first average is held to 30 ms. TrueHD (`TrueHDSyncScale.h`, 0.45 in one place): a correction is counted in the unit of its error; a packet left out is not counted as a frame in the sink and moves the readings of the next average; a correction longer than its pool of 500 ms holds the stream until its pauses come; and during the measure of a start or a seek TrueHD goes to the sink at its water level. Tested on the host with a harness of the audio engine (961 checks).
- DTS: `MAX_IEC61937_PACKET` holds the largest burst (65536 bytes); `PackDTS()` and `PackDTSHD()` read an odd-sized frame no further than its end; a change of the DTS period opens the sink again; the parser does not wait for a DTS-HD frame larger than itself. tanio99's two commits of Kodi pull request 30440 and heit88's of pull request 30326 are taken with their authors, with a test of the codec family on TrueHD made by FFmpeg.

**Discs and files**

- Films in the fields of interlaced video: `FieldCadence.h` labels each decoded picture against the one before (how much its top and bottom fields changed; how much it combs as coded and with its first field woven with the second field of the picture before) and tells a run: 3:2 where a top and a bottom field repeat at their own places of the cycle of five in nine cycles of ten and nowhere else; 25 in 30 where a frame repeats at one place of the cycle of six; 2:2 where nine weaves in ten are the frame's own (or one field off) and no field repeats alone; video where pictures comb both ways. `Decide()` says film only where every place that told agrees and none says video. `FieldCadenceProbe.h` decodes the places (the demuxer's packets of the start; 2 MiB at 50, 10 and 75 % of a file; the same places of each DVD title in `CDiscSoftTelecineScan`). The decoder's graph is FFmpeg's `fieldmatch=order=auto:combmatch=full,bwdif=mode=send_frame:parity=auto:deint=interlaced`, with `decimate=dupthresh=10:scthresh=100` for 3:2 and `decimate=cycle=6:dupthresh=10:scthresh=100` for 25 in 30; such a stream is decoded by the processor. `CFilmTimer::SetFilmPace()` times the pictures at the film's pace, and `CCadenceWatch` says in `kodi.log` where the cadence stops and comes back. 128 of 128 clips of the probe's corpus told right.
- `CDVDDemuxFFmpeg::SetCancelCheck()` takes a function that `Aborted()` calls beside the abort flag and the timeout, so that the open, the probe and the read of a file that does not answer end when whoever waits for them wants. `CDVDFileInfo::GetFileStreamDetails()` and `GetFileDuration()` take a flag for it, the loader of a video list gives the probe its stop, and `StopThread()` of the loader waits on an event and, after 300 ms, writes one line with the item it is at.
- A network stall: `CFileCache` waits out a source of known length that stops before its end for a reader that asks it (`IOControl::READ_WAIT`, the player, which can stop at once with `READ_ABORT`), reopens it at the byte it had reached, ends with an error after 300 s without data or at a 4xx answer, and takes 416 for the end; the demuxer gives the time of such a wait back to its 20-second deadline; the player buffers meanwhile, with the clock stopped, and shows the notice after 5 s. `CCurlFile` returns the data that a read asked for without another wait, `Stop` aborts a read that waits for its source, `CSaveFileState` no longer asks the server for a date, and `CHTTPDirectory` no longer reads a file whole to find that it is not a directory. Host tests under AddressSanitizer and ThreadSanitizer (the read position and the end of the input are atomics now) and a virtual machine with a server that stops.
- `Mpeg2SoftTelecine.h` reads the sequence extension and the picture headers of a packet and decides, without decoding, whether a stream has the cadence of soft telecine: the pictures of each complete group are put in display order, and a window of 24 must be progressive frames of a non-progressive sequence at 30000/1001 or 30 with `repeat_first_field` on every second one. A file is sampled at its middle, and at 10 % and 75 % where the middle and the start disagree, headers only, with a handle of its own; over the internet, and after 1.5 s, the start decides. `CDiscSoftTelecineScan` reads the titles of two minutes or more of a DVD image or folder with a second libdvdnav, 3 MiB or 4 s at most each, and closing Kodi never waits for it. `FilmTimer.h` gives the pictures their times.
- The library: at the end of a playback, `CSaveFileState` adds to the stream details what the player saw and they lack (the profile and the layer of profile 7, HDR10+ in the pictures, the HDR10+ or HLG of a Dolby Vision stream), in Kodi's own columns, never over details from an NFO; `CDVDFileInfo::StrmTarget()` reads a `.strm` that names one file of this system or of the home network, without `#KODIPROP`, as that file when it is scanned. `VideoPicture::Reset()` clears the other HDR type and the layer of each picture.
- Blu-ray: `libbluray-9089` (the marks of a seek's position after its target), `9090` (a new play item told before the bytes of its clip, with Kodi's `BlurayJoin.h` telling a seamless join by where the read began), `9091` and Kodi's `BlurayPreroll.h` (the primary video from the random-access picture before IN, decoded and not presented, also by the DRM PRIME decoder), `9092` (the filter's PIDs in the order that wipes the subtitles before IN with menus too), `9093` (the enhancement layer's picture before an entry point, by DTS, within 1.5 s of ATS and 24 MiB), `9098` (that look reads its units in runs, with a check at compile time that its first run fits its buffer) and `9094` (a seek forgets that the reader passed the OUT time). Played directly, a join of connection condition 1 keeps its bytes and opens the demuxer again (`NEXTSTREAM_OPEN`). `libbluray-9096` keeps a BD-J stream or angle set before a playlist player has a play item; `libbluray-9097` shows a BD-J graphics plane that has waited 100 ms at its next tick; `libbluray-9099` reads an image that the player reads itself in aligned chunks of 64 KiB and keeps the last 4 MiB (`BLURAY_PLAYER_SETTING_IMAGE_CACHE`, which Kodi sets only for an image that is not on a local disk), and Kodi's `ReadBlocks()` reads whole blocks where a delivery ends inside one. The menus of an HDR playlist are HDR overlays in PQ, BT.2020 (`BlurayGraphicsRange.h`, from the dynamic range type of the playlist's STN table), and the palette of a disc's graphics is decoded in the matrix of its video. matthane's `e5a50069a8e0` and `63362443735b` of Kodi's `master` are taken as they are.
